I guess… it’s my eating, mostly. I mean, I have a pretty demanding job, marketing, and there are always client dinners and catered meetings, so food is just kind of around all the time—but that’s not really the point.

What scares me is at night, after everyone goes to bed, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have some leftovers, and then suddenly I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta, the dessert I brought back from a client dinner, and whatever snacks were in the pantry, all in one sitting. I don’t really know how to explain it, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.

I don’t really know how to explain it, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How often do these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… pretty regular, I guess. For the last three months or so, there’s usually at least one night almost every week where it happens. Usually after I’ve had an argument or I’m coming home late from some work thing.\n\nSome weeks it’s more like twice, especially if work’s been chaotic. I keep telling myself it’s just because there’s leftover catering or I’m tired, but… yeah, it’s been almost weekly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel out of control. Not at the very first bite, maybe, but once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that makes decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for something else. It’s ridiculous because I can make decisions all day at work, you know, budgets and clients and everyone needing me to have an answer, but then at home, in the kitchen, I just can’t seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Afterward I feel… embarrassed, honestly, and kind of disgusted with myself. I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—partly because I don’t want to know, if I’m being truthful.

I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, actively staging a cover-up, if that’s what you mean. But I also don’t exactly announce it.

Not at the very first bite, maybe, but once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that makes decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for something else. It’s ridiculous because I can make decisions all day at work, you know, budgets and clients and everyone needing me to have an answer, but then at home, in the kitchen, I just can’t seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Afterward I feel… embarrassed, honestly, and kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’ve completely ruined this image I try so hard to keep up—being disciplined, organized, the person who has everything under control.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there thinking, why didn’t I just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else? Which is stupid, because I even have a yoga mat in the den that I bought during one of those phases where I thought I was going to become a “morning routine” person. But instead I’m just there feeling awful about what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t even really sit down most of the time, which probably makes it worse. I’ll be standing at the kitchen island, or half-looking at work emails on my phone, and I’m just eating so fast I barely taste any of it.\n\nIt’s strange because with clients I’m always telling people to “slow the process down” and “be intentional”—sorry, that’s such a marketing-manager thing to say—but then I’ll look down and the containers are just empty. Like I haven’t really registered what I was even eating until it’s already gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—partly because I don’t want to know, if I’m being truthful.\n\nMy clothes… maybe they fit a little differently some days, but then I’ll tell myself it’s just laundry or stress bloating or whatever. I know that’s not a very clear answer. I used to track everything for a while, back when I had one of those fitness apps, and then it became this whole thing where I was checking it more than my work calendar, which is saying something because my calendar is basically my second brain.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a “significant” change. I haven’t really checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal the fact that you've eaten all this food during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, actively staging a cover-up, if that’s what you mean. But I also don’t exactly announce it.\n\nSometimes I’ll throw packaging away right away or straighten up the kitchen, but honestly I straighten everything up—my desk, my inbox, the pantry shelves if I’m procrastinating—so I don’t know if that “counts” as hiding or if it’s just me being particular. My team jokes that my color-coded project boards could survive a natural disaster, which is… not really relevant, sorry.\n\nSo, I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think anyone is really around to notice most of the time, so I haven’t thought of it as concealing it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable afterward sometimes, like physically and mentally, and then I’ll start doing that spiral where I’m thinking, “Okay, tomorrow I’ll be more structured,” and then I make some overly ambitious plan, like I’m launching a whole product strategy for my own life—which is ridiculous.\n\nBut vomiting or laxatives… I don’t have a clear “yes” there. I’m not sure if you mean ever-ever, or as a pattern after these episodes. It’s not something I’ve noticed myself doing after them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think so. Not every single time, maybe, but a lot of the worst ones are after a tense night with my husband. Like if we’ve had that kind of cold, clipped conversation where nobody is technically yelling but the whole room feels awful.

And then once I start eating, it’s like… the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for more. Which is embarrassing to say out loud.

It’s strange because at work I make decisions all day—budgets, campaigns, people wanting answers immediately—and I can be very composed. But then at night, after something upsetting, it’s like I can’t get that same part of myself to show up.

I keep telling myself it’s just because there’s leftover catering or I’m tired, but… yeah, it’s been almost weekly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel out of control. Not at the very first bite, maybe, but once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that makes decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for something else. It’s ridiculous because I can make decisions all day at work, you know, budgets and clients and everyone needing me to have an answer, but then at home, in the kitchen, I just can’t seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Afterward I feel… embarrassed, honestly, and kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’ve completely ruined this image I try so hard to keep up—being disciplined, organized, the person who has everything under control.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there thinking, why didn’t I just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else? Which is stupid, because I even have a yoga mat in the den that I bought during one of those phases where I thought I was going to become a “morning routine” person. But instead I’m just there feeling awful about what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t even really sit down most of the time, which probably makes it worse. I’ll be standing at the kitchen island, or half-looking at work emails on my phone, and I’m just eating so fast I barely taste any of it.\n\nIt’s strange because with clients I’m always telling people to “slow the process down” and “be intentional”—sorry, that’s such a marketing-manager thing to say—but then I’ll look down and the containers are just empty. Like I haven’t really registered what I was even eating until it’s already gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—partly because I don’t want to know, if I’m being truthful.\n\nMy clothes… maybe they fit a little differently some days, but then I’ll tell myself it’s just laundry or stress bloating or whatever. I know that’s not a very clear answer. I used to track everything for a while, back when I had one of those fitness apps, and then it became this whole thing where I was checking it more than my work calendar, which is saying something because my calendar is basically my second brain.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a “significant” change. I haven’t really checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal the fact that you've eaten all this food during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. My team jokes that my color-coded project boards could survive a natural disaster, which is… not really relevant, sorry.\n\nSo, I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think anyone is really around to notice most of the time, so I haven’t thought of it as concealing it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I’m not sure if you mean ever-ever, or as a pattern after these episodes. It’s not something I’ve noticed myself doing after them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively after one of these nighttime eating episodes to try to burn off the calories?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a deliberate “I have to burn this off” way, at least. I mean, sometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more active, but then that turns into me reorganizing my schedule or looking up workout plans and comparing apps instead of actually doing anything, which is very me.\n\nI used to have phases where I’d get intense about steps or classes, but after these nighttime episodes specifically? I haven’t really noticed myself exercising afterward. Not every single time, maybe, but a lot of the worst ones are after a tense night with my husband. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s my eating, mostly. I mean, I have a pretty demanding job, marketing, and there are always client dinners and catered meetings, so food is just kind of around all the time—but that’s not really the point.\n\nWhat scares me is at night, after everyone goes to bed, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have some leftovers, and then suddenly I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta, the dessert I brought back from a client dinner, and whatever snacks were in the pantry, all in one sitting. I don’t really know how to explain it, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How often do these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… pretty regular, I guess. For the last three months or so, there’s usually at least one night almost every week where it happens. I keep telling myself it’s just because there’s leftover catering or I’m tired, but… yeah, it’s been almost weekly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel out of control. Not at the very first bite, maybe, but once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that makes decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for something else. It’s ridiculous because I can make decisions all day at work, you know, budgets and clients and everyone needing me to have an answer, but then at home, in the kitchen, I just can’t seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Afterward I feel… embarrassed, honestly, and kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’ve completely ruined this image I try so hard to keep up—being disciplined, organized, the person who has everything under control.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there thinking, why didn’t I just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else? Which is stupid, because I even have a yoga mat in the den that I bought during one of those phases where I thought I was going to become a “morning routine” person. But instead I’m just there feeling awful about what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t even really sit down most of the time, which probably makes it worse. I’ll be standing at the kitchen island, or half-looking at work emails on my phone, and I’m just eating so fast I barely taste any of it.\n\nIt’s strange because with clients I’m always telling people to “slow the process down” and “be intentional”—sorry, that’s such a marketing-manager thing to say—but then I’ll look down and the containers are just empty. Like I haven’t really registered what I was even eating until it’s already gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—partly because I don’t want to know, if I’m being truthful.\n\nMy clothes… maybe they fit a little differently some days, but then I’ll tell myself it’s just laundry or stress bloating or whatever. I know that’s not a very clear answer. I used to track everything for a while, back when I had one of those fitness apps, and then it became this whole thing where I was checking it more than my work calendar, which is saying something because my calendar is basically my second brain.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a “significant” change. I haven’t really checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal the fact that you've eaten all this food during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, actively staging a cover-up, if that’s what you mean. But I also don’t exactly announce it.\n\nSometimes I’ll throw packaging away right away or straighten up the kitchen, but honestly I straighten everything up—my desk, my inbox, the pantry shelves if I’m procrastinating—so I don’t know if that “counts” as hiding or if it’s just me being particular. My team jokes that my color-coded project boards could survive a natural disaster, which is… not really relevant, sorry.\n\nSo, I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think anyone is really around to notice most of the time, so I haven’t thought of it as concealing it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable afterward sometimes, like physically and mentally, and then I’ll start doing that spiral where I’m thinking, “Okay, tomorrow I’ll be more structured,” and then I make some overly ambitious plan, like I’m launching a whole product strategy for my own life—which is ridiculous.\n\nBut vomiting or laxatives… I don’t have a clear “yes” there. I’m not sure if you mean ever-ever, or as a pattern after these episodes. It’s not something I’ve noticed myself doing after them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively after one of these nighttime eating episodes to try to burn off the calories?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a deliberate “I have to burn this off” way, at least. I mean, sometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more active, but then that turns into me reorganizing my schedule or looking up workout plans and comparing apps instead of actually doing anything, which is very me.\n\nI used to have phases where I’d get intense about steps or classes, but after these nighttime episodes specifically? I haven’t really noticed myself exercising afterward. Usually it’s late, and I’m tired, and then I’m more likely to just… sit with feeling bad about it than go do a workout.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at those times because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single time, maybe, but a lot of the worst ones are after a tense night with my husband. Like if we’ve had that kind of cold, clipped conversation where nobody is technically yelling but the whole room feels awful.\n\nAnd then once I start eating, it’s like… the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for more. Which is embarrassing to say out loud.\n\nIt’s strange because at work I make decisions all day—budgets, campaigns, people wanting answers immediately—and I can be very composed. But then at night, after something upsetting, it’s like I can’t get that same part of myself to show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed, especially after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… afterward, definitely. I’m not sure… I don’t think so? Like, I don’t usually find a wrapper and have no memory of eating it, if that’s what you mean.

Sometimes the whole thing feels kind of blurry afterward, but more in that way where I wish I hadn’t been doing it, not like I blacked out. I’ll remember the general… the standing in the kitchen, opening things, picking at something. Although then I start thinking about how many times I open the pantry for completely unrelated reasons, like looking for the dog’s treats or realizing we’re out of paper towels, and it all gets mixed together in my head.

But no, I don’t think I’ve had the experience of eating something and truly not remembering it.

Yeah… that’s probably the worst part. Once I start, especially if it’s been one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that’s supposed to make decisions just sort of… shuts off.

I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for more anyway. It’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing, exactly. I do. But in that moment it feels really hard to pull myself out of it.

And then I start thinking about how ridiculous it is because at work I can manage ten different deadlines and a whole team, and then at home I can’t seem to stop myself in front of the pantry. So yes, I do feel unable to stop once it gets going.

I’ll be standing at the kitchen island, or half-looking at work emails on my phone, and I’m just eating so fast I barely taste any of it.\n\nIt’s strange because with clients I’m always telling people to “slow the process down” and “be intentional”—sorry, that’s such a marketing-manager thing to say—but then I’ll look down and the containers are just empty. Like I haven’t really registered what I was even eating until it’s already gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—partly because I don’t want to know, if I’m being truthful.\n\nMy clothes… maybe they fit a little differently some days, but then I’ll tell myself it’s just laundry or stress bloating or whatever. I know that’s not a very clear answer. I used to track everything for a while, back when I had one of those fitness apps, and then it became this whole thing where I was checking it more than my work calendar, which is saying something because my calendar is basically my second brain.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a “significant” change. I haven’t really checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal the fact that you've eaten all this food during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, actively staging a cover-up, if that’s what you mean. But I also don’t exactly announce it.\n\nSometimes I’ll throw packaging away right away or straighten up the kitchen, but honestly I straighten everything up—my desk, my inbox, the pantry shelves if I’m procrastinating—so I don’t know if that “counts” as hiding or if it’s just me being particular. My team jokes that my color-coded project boards could survive a natural disaster, which is… not really relevant, sorry.\n\nSo, I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think anyone is really around to notice most of the time, so I haven’t thought of it as concealing it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable afterward sometimes, like physically and mentally, and then I’ll start doing that spiral where I’m thinking, “Okay, tomorrow I’ll be more structured,” and then I make some overly ambitious plan, like I’m launching a whole product strategy for my own life—which is ridiculous.\n\nBut vomiting or laxatives… I don’t have a clear “yes” there. I’m not sure if you mean ever-ever, or as a pattern after these episodes. It’s not something I’ve noticed myself doing after them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively after one of these nighttime eating episodes to try to burn off the calories?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a deliberate “I have to burn this off” way, at least. I mean, sometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more active, but then that turns into me reorganizing my schedule or looking up workout plans and comparing apps instead of actually doing anything, which is very me.\n\nI used to have phases where I’d get intense about steps or classes, but after these nighttime episodes specifically? I haven’t really noticed myself exercising afterward. Usually it’s late, and I’m tired, and then I’m more likely to just… sit with feeling bad about it than go do a workout.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at those times because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single time, maybe, but a lot of the worst ones are after a tense night with my husband. Like if we’ve had that kind of cold, clipped conversation where nobody is technically yelling but the whole room feels awful.\n\nAnd then once I start eating, it’s like… the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for more. Which is embarrassing to say out loud.\n\nIt’s strange because at work I make decisions all day—budgets, campaigns, people wanting answers immediately—and I can be very composed. But then at night, after something upsetting, it’s like I can’t get that same part of myself to show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed, especially after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… afterward, definitely. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed in the big official sense, but I feel really low and embarrassed. Kind of disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ve ruined this whole idea I have of myself as a disciplined person, which is probably dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes. I’ll sit there thinking, “Why didn’t you just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else?” And then I start thinking about all the people at work who see me as organized and put together, and I’m sitting in the kitchen feeling like I’ve completely failed at something very basic.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you've eaten something during one of these episodes and then don't remember eating it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so? Like, I don’t usually find a wrapper and have no memory of eating it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes the whole thing feels kind of blurry afterward, but more in that way where I wish I hadn’t been doing it, not like I blacked out. I’ll remember the general… the standing in the kitchen, opening things, picking at something. Although then I start thinking about how many times I open the pantry for completely unrelated reasons, like looking for the dog’s treats or realizing we’re out of paper towels, and it all gets mixed together in my head.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve had the experience of eating something and truly not remembering it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed right as you're finishing eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially right as it’s ending, or just after. I get this awful embarrassed feeling, like, “What did I just do?” It’s not even only about the food, it’s like I’ve blown this whole image I have of being disciplined and in control.\n\nAnd then my mind starts doing that annoying comparison thing—like, I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or just gone upstairs and brushed my teeth, you know? I’ll sit there feeling kind of disgusted with myself. Which sounds harsh, but that’s honestly where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the worst part. Once I start, especially if it’s been one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that’s supposed to make decisions just sort of… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for more anyway. It’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing, exactly. I do. But in that moment it feels really hard to pull myself out of it.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about how ridiculous it is because at work I can manage ten different deadlines and a whole team, and then at home I can’t seem to stop myself in front of the pantry. So yes, I do feel unable to stop once it gets going.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. It’s like once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, the decision-making part of my brain just goes offline.

I’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop, that’s enough,” and then my hand is still going back for more. And it’s not even always that I wanted that specific thing, if that makes sense… it’s more like I’m just in the pantry or kitchen and continuing. Like I’ve lost the thread of what I meant to do.

Sorry, I know I’m probably over-explaining. But yes, in those moments I don’t feel in control of what or how much I’m eating.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s my eating, mostly. I mean, I have a pretty demanding job, marketing, and there are always client dinners and catered meetings, so food is just kind of around all the time—but that’s not really the point.\n\nWhat scares me is at night, after everyone goes to bed, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have some leftovers, and then suddenly I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta, the dessert I brought back from a client dinner, and whatever snacks were in the pantry, all in one sitting. I don’t really know how to explain it, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How often do these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… pretty regular, I guess. For the last three months or so, there’s usually at least one night almost every week where it happens. Usually after I’ve had an argument or I’m coming home late from some work thing.\n\nSome weeks it’s more like twice, especially if work’s been chaotic. I keep telling myself it’s just because there’s leftover catering or I’m tired, but… yeah, it’s been almost weekly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel out of control. Not at the very first bite, maybe, but once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that makes decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for something else. It’s ridiculous because I can make decisions all day at work, you know, budgets and clients and everyone needing me to have an answer, but then at home, in the kitchen, I just can’t seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Afterward I feel… embarrassed, honestly, and kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’ve completely ruined this image I try so hard to keep up—being disciplined, organized, the person who has everything under control.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there thinking, why didn’t I just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else? Which is stupid, because I even have a yoga mat in the den that I bought during one of those phases where I thought I was going to become a “morning routine” person. But instead I’m just there feeling awful about what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t even really sit down most of the time, which probably makes it worse. I’ll be standing at the kitchen island, or half-looking at work emails on my phone, and I’m just eating so fast I barely taste any of it.\n\nIt’s strange because with clients I’m always telling people to “slow the process down” and “be intentional”—sorry, that’s such a marketing-manager thing to say—but then I’ll look down and the containers are just empty. Like I haven’t really registered what I was even eating until it’s already gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—partly because I don’t want to know, if I’m being truthful.\n\nMy clothes… maybe they fit a little differently some days, but then I’ll tell myself it’s just laundry or stress bloating or whatever. I know that’s not a very clear answer. I used to track everything for a while, back when I had one of those fitness apps, and then it became this whole thing where I was checking it more than my work calendar, which is saying something because my calendar is basically my second brain.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a “significant” change. I haven’t really checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal the fact that you've eaten all this food during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, actively staging a cover-up, if that’s what you mean. But I also don’t exactly announce it.\n\nSometimes I’ll throw packaging away right away or straighten up the kitchen, but honestly I straighten everything up—my desk, my inbox, the pantry shelves if I’m procrastinating—so I don’t know if that “counts” as hiding or if it’s just me being particular. My team jokes that my color-coded project boards could survive a natural disaster, which is… not really relevant, sorry.\n\nSo, I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think anyone is really around to notice most of the time, so I haven’t thought of it as concealing it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable afterward sometimes, like physically and mentally, and then I’ll start doing that spiral where I’m thinking, “Okay, tomorrow I’ll be more structured,” and then I make some overly ambitious plan, like I’m launching a whole product strategy for my own life—which is ridiculous.\n\nBut vomiting or laxatives… I don’t have a clear “yes” there. I’m not sure if you mean ever-ever, or as a pattern after these episodes. It’s not something I’ve noticed myself doing after them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively after one of these nighttime eating episodes to try to burn off the calories?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a deliberate “I have to burn this off” way, at least. I mean, sometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more active, but then that turns into me reorganizing my schedule or looking up workout plans and comparing apps instead of actually doing anything, which is very me.\n\nI used to have phases where I’d get intense about steps or classes, but after these nighttime episodes specifically? I haven’t really noticed myself exercising afterward. Usually it’s late, and I’m tired, and then I’m more likely to just… sit with feeling bad about it than go do a workout.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at those times because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single time, maybe, but a lot of the worst ones are after a tense night with my husband. Like if we’ve had that kind of cold, clipped conversation where nobody is technically yelling but the whole room feels awful.\n\nAnd then once I start eating, it’s like… the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for more. Which is embarrassing to say out loud.\n\nIt’s strange because at work I make decisions all day—budgets, campaigns, people wanting answers immediately—and I can be very composed. But then at night, after something upsetting, it’s like I can’t get that same part of myself to show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed, especially after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… afterward, definitely. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed in the big official sense, but I feel really low and embarrassed. Kind of disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ve ruined this whole idea I have of myself as a disciplined person, which is probably dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes. I’ll sit there thinking, “Why didn’t you just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else?” And then I start thinking about all the people at work who see me as organized and put together, and I’m sitting in the kitchen feeling like I’ve completely failed at something very basic.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you've eaten something during one of these episodes and then don't remember eating it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so? Like, I don’t usually find a wrapper and have no memory of eating it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes the whole thing feels kind of blurry afterward, but more in that way where I wish I hadn’t been doing it, not like I blacked out. I’ll remember the general… the standing in the kitchen, opening things, picking at something. Although then I start thinking about how many times I open the pantry for completely unrelated reasons, like looking for the dog’s treats or realizing we’re out of paper towels, and it all gets mixed together in my head.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve had the experience of eating something and truly not remembering it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed right as you're finishing eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially right as it’s ending, or just after. I’ll sit there feeling kind of disgusted with myself. Which sounds harsh, but that’s honestly where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the worst part. Once I start, especially if it’s been one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that’s supposed to make decisions just sort of… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for more anyway. I’ll sit there feeling kind of disgusted with myself. It’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing, exactly. I do. But in that moment it feels really hard to pull myself out of it.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about how ridiculous it is because at work I can manage ten different deadlines and a whole team, and then at home I can’t seem to stop myself in front of the pantry. So yes, I do feel unable to stop once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, the decision-making part of my brain just goes offline.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop, that’s enough,” and then my hand is still going back for more. I mean… maybe? I’m not totally sure I can say it’s usually that. The arguments definitely leave me feeling wound up, but I haven’t really tracked it in a neat way, you know?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s my eating, mostly. I mean, I have a pretty demanding job, marketing, and there are always client dinners and catered meetings, so food is just kind of around all the time—but that’s not really the point.\n\nWhat scares me is at night, after everyone goes to bed, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have some leftovers, and then suddenly I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta, the dessert I brought back from a client dinner, and whatever snacks were in the pantry, all in one sitting. I don’t really know how to explain it, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How often do these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… pretty regular, I guess. For the last three months or so, there’s usually at least one night almost every week where it happens. Usually after I’ve had an argument or I’m coming home late from some work thing.\n\nSome weeks it’s more like twice, especially if work’s been chaotic. I keep telling myself it’s just because there’s leftover catering or I’m tired, but… yeah, it’s been almost weekly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel out of control. Not at the very first bite, maybe, but once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that makes decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for something else. It’s ridiculous because I can make decisions all day at work, you know, budgets and clients and everyone needing me to have an answer, but then at home, in the kitchen, I just can’t seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Afterward I feel… embarrassed, honestly, and kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’ve completely ruined this image I try so hard to keep up—being disciplined, organized, the person who has everything under control.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there thinking, why didn’t I just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else? Which is stupid, because I even have a yoga mat in the den that I bought during one of those phases where I thought I was going to become a “morning routine” person. But instead I’m just there feeling awful about what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t even really sit down most of the time, which probably makes it worse. I’ll be standing at the kitchen island, or half-looking at work emails on my phone, and I’m just eating so fast I barely taste any of it.\n\nIt’s strange because with clients I’m always telling people to “slow the process down” and “be intentional”—sorry, that’s such a marketing-manager thing to say—but then I’ll look down and the containers are just empty. Like I haven’t really registered what I was even eating until it’s already gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—partly because I don’t want to know, if I’m being truthful.\n\nMy clothes… maybe they fit a little differently some days, but then I’ll tell myself it’s just laundry or stress bloating or whatever. I know that’s not a very clear answer. I used to track everything for a while, back when I had one of those fitness apps, and then it became this whole thing where I was checking it more than my work calendar, which is saying something because my calendar is basically my second brain.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a “significant” change. I haven’t really checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal the fact that you've eaten all this food during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, actively staging a cover-up, if that’s what you mean. But I also don’t exactly announce it.\n\nSometimes I’ll throw packaging away right away or straighten up the kitchen, but honestly I straighten everything up—my desk, my inbox, the pantry shelves if I’m procrastinating—so I don’t know if that “counts” as hiding or if it’s just me being particular. My team jokes that my color-coded project boards could survive a natural disaster, which is… not really relevant, sorry.\n\nSo, I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think anyone is really around to notice most of the time, so I haven’t thought of it as concealing it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable afterward sometimes, like physically and mentally, and then I’ll start doing that spiral where I’m thinking, “Okay, tomorrow I’ll be more structured,” and then I make some overly ambitious plan, like I’m launching a whole product strategy for my own life—which is ridiculous.\n\nBut vomiting or laxatives… I don’t have a clear “yes” there. I’m not sure if you mean ever-ever, or as a pattern after these episodes. It’s not something I’ve noticed myself doing after them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively after one of these nighttime eating episodes to try to burn off the calories?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a deliberate “I have to burn this off” way, at least. I mean, sometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more active, but then that turns into me reorganizing my schedule or looking up workout plans and comparing apps instead of actually doing anything, which is very me.\n\nI used to have phases where I’d get intense about steps or classes, but after these nighttime episodes specifically? I haven’t really noticed myself exercising afterward. Usually it’s late, and I’m tired, and then I’m more likely to just… sit with feeling bad about it than go do a workout.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at those times because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single time, maybe, but a lot of the worst ones are after a tense night with my husband. Like if we’ve had that kind of cold, clipped conversation where nobody is technically yelling but the whole room feels awful.\n\nAnd then once I start eating, it’s like… the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for more. Which is embarrassing to say out loud.\n\nIt’s strange because at work I make decisions all day—budgets, campaigns, people wanting answers immediately—and I can be very composed. But then at night, after something upsetting, it’s like I can’t get that same part of myself to show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed, especially after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… afterward, definitely. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed in the big official sense, but I feel really low and embarrassed. Kind of disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ve ruined this whole idea I have of myself as a disciplined person, which is probably dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes. I’ll sit there thinking, “Why didn’t you just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else?” And then I start thinking about all the people at work who see me as organized and put together, and I’m sitting in the kitchen feeling like I’ve completely failed at something very basic.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you've eaten something during one of these episodes and then don't remember eating it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so? Like, I don’t usually find a wrapper and have no memory of eating it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes the whole thing feels kind of blurry afterward, but more in that way where I wish I hadn’t been doing it, not like I blacked out. I’ll remember the general… the standing in the kitchen, opening things, picking at something. Although then I start thinking about how many times I open the pantry for completely unrelated reasons, like looking for the dog’s treats or realizing we’re out of paper towels, and it all gets mixed together in my head.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve had the experience of eating something and truly not remembering it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed right as you're finishing eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially right as it’s ending, or just after. I get this awful embarrassed feeling, like, “What did I just do?” It’s not even only about the food, it’s like I’ve blown this whole image I have of being disciplined and in control.\n\nAnd then my mind starts doing that annoying comparison thing—like, I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or just gone upstairs and brushed my teeth, you know? I’ll sit there feeling kind of disgusted with myself. Which sounds harsh, but that’s honestly where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the worst part. Once I start, especially if it’s been one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that’s supposed to make decisions just sort of… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for more anyway. It’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing, exactly. I do. But yes, in those moments I don’t feel in control of what or how much I’m eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the stress or anxiety you feel, especially after arguments with your husband, is what usually triggers these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I’m not totally sure I can say it’s usually that. And then if there was tension with my husband, it’s sort of… added on top. But I don’t know if I can honestly say, “Yes, that’s the trigger every time.”\n\nI probably should pay more attention to it. I’m the kind of person who makes spreadsheets for campaigns and client timelines, but apparently not for my own life, which is ridiculous. So… I guess my answer is I’m not sure, but stress seems like it might be part of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ll sit there thinking, “Why didn’t you just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else?” And then I start thinking about all the people at work who see me as organized and put together, and I’m sitting in the kitchen feeling like I’ve completely failed at something very basic.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you've eaten something during one of these episodes and then don't remember eating it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so? Like, I don’t usually find a wrapper and have no memory of eating it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes the whole thing feels kind of blurry afterward, but more in that way where I wish I hadn’t been doing it, not like I blacked out. I’ll remember the general… the standing in the kitchen, opening things, picking at something. Although then I start thinking about how many times I open the pantry for completely unrelated reasons, like looking for the dog’s treats or realizing we’re out of paper towels, and it all gets mixed together in my head.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve had the experience of eating something and truly not remembering it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed right as you're finishing eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially right as it’s ending, or just after. I keep telling myself it’s just because there’s leftover catering or I’m tired, but… yeah, it’s been almost weekly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel out of control. Not at the very first bite, maybe, but once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that makes decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for something else. It’s ridiculous because I can make decisions all day at work, you know, budgets and clients and everyone needing me to have an answer, but then at home, in the kitchen, I just can’t seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Afterward I feel… embarrassed, honestly, and kind of disgusted with myself. Like I’ve completely ruined this image I try so hard to keep up—being disciplined, organized, the person who has everything under control.\n\nAnd then I’ll sit there thinking, why didn’t I just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else? Which is stupid, because I even have a yoga mat in the den that I bought during one of those phases where I thought I was going to become a “morning routine” person. But instead I’m just there feeling awful about what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t even really sit down most of the time, which probably makes it worse. I’ll be standing at the kitchen island, or half-looking at work emails on my phone, and I’m just eating so fast I barely taste any of it.\n\nIt’s strange because with clients I’m always telling people to “slow the process down” and “be intentional”—sorry, that’s such a marketing-manager thing to say—but then I’ll look down and the containers are just empty. Like I haven’t really registered what I was even eating until it’s already gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—partly because I don’t want to know, if I’m being truthful.\n\nMy clothes… maybe they fit a little differently some days, but then I’ll tell myself it’s just laundry or stress bloating or whatever. I know that’s not a very clear answer. I used to track everything for a while, back when I had one of those fitness apps, and then it became this whole thing where I was checking it more than my work calendar, which is saying something because my calendar is basically my second brain.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a “significant” change. I haven’t really checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal the fact that you've eaten all this food during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’m, like, actively staging a cover-up, if that’s what you mean. But I also don’t exactly announce it.\n\nSometimes I’ll throw packaging away right away or straighten up the kitchen, but honestly I straighten everything up—my desk, my inbox, the pantry shelves if I’m procrastinating—so I don’t know if that “counts” as hiding or if it’s just me being particular. My team jokes that my color-coded project boards could survive a natural disaster, which is… not really relevant, sorry.\n\nSo, I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think anyone is really around to notice most of the time, so I haven’t thought of it as concealing it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable afterward sometimes, like physically and mentally, and then I’ll start doing that spiral where I’m thinking, “Okay, tomorrow I’ll be more structured,” and then I make some overly ambitious plan, like I’m launching a whole product strategy for my own life—which is ridiculous.\n\nBut vomiting or laxatives… I don’t have a clear “yes” there. I’m not sure if you mean ever-ever, or as a pattern after these episodes. It’s not something I’ve noticed myself doing after them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively after one of these nighttime eating episodes to try to burn off the calories?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a deliberate “I have to burn this off” way, at least. I mean, sometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more active, but then that turns into me reorganizing my schedule or looking up workout plans and comparing apps instead of actually doing anything, which is very me.\n\nI used to have phases where I’d get intense about steps or classes, but after these nighttime episodes specifically? I haven’t really noticed myself exercising afterward. Usually it’s late, and I’m tired, and then I’m more likely to just… sit with feeling bad about it than go do a workout.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at those times because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single time, maybe, but a lot of the worst ones are after a tense night with my husband. Like if we’ve had that kind of cold, clipped conversation where nobody is technically yelling but the whole room feels awful.\n\nAnd then once I start eating, it’s like… the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for more. Which is embarrassing to say out loud.\n\nIt’s strange because at work I make decisions all day—budgets, campaigns, people wanting answers immediately—and I can be very composed. But then at night, after something upsetting, it’s like I can’t get that same part of myself to show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed, especially after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… afterward, definitely. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed in the big official sense, but I feel really low and embarrassed. Kind of disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ve ruined this whole idea I have of myself as a disciplined person, which is probably dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes. I’ll sit there thinking, “Why didn’t you just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else?” And then I start thinking about all the people at work who see me as organized and put together, and I’m sitting in the kitchen feeling like I’ve completely failed at something very basic.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you've eaten something during one of these episodes and then don't remember eating it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so? Like, I don’t usually find a wrapper and have no memory of eating it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes the whole thing feels kind of blurry afterward, but more in that way where I wish I hadn’t been doing it, not like I blacked out. I’ll remember the general… the standing in the kitchen, opening things, picking at something. Although then I start thinking about how many times I open the pantry for completely unrelated reasons, like looking for the dog’s treats or realizing we’re out of paper towels, and it all gets mixed together in my head.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve had the experience of eating something and truly not remembering it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed right as you're finishing eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially right as it’s ending, or just after. I get this awful embarrassed feeling, like, “What did I just do?” It’s not even only about the food, it’s like I’ve blown this whole image I have of being disciplined and in control.\n\nAnd then my mind starts doing that annoying comparison thing—like, I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or just gone upstairs and brushed my teeth, you know? I’ll sit there feeling kind of disgusted with myself. Which sounds harsh, but that’s honestly where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the worst part. Once I start, especially if it’s been one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that’s supposed to make decisions just sort of… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for more anyway. It’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing, exactly. I do. But in that moment it feels really hard to pull myself out of it.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about how ridiculous it is because at work I can manage ten different deadlines and a whole team, and then at home I can’t seem to stop myself in front of the pantry. So yes, I do feel unable to stop once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, the decision-making part of my brain just goes offline.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop, that’s enough,” and then my hand is still going back for more. And it’s not even always that I wanted that specific thing, if that makes sense… it’s more like I’m just in the pantry or kitchen and continuing. Like I’ve lost the thread of what I meant to do.\n\nSorry, I know I’m probably over-explaining. But yes, in those moments I don’t feel in control of what or how much I’m eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the stress or anxiety you feel, especially after arguments with your husband, is what usually triggers these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I’m not totally sure I can say it’s usually that. The arguments definitely leave me feeling wound up, but I haven’t really tracked it in a neat way, you know?\n\nSometimes by nighttime I’m just carrying the whole day around with me—work stuff, little household things, that feeling like I didn’t get a minute to myself. And then if there was tension with my husband, it’s sort of… added on top. But I don’t know if I can honestly say, “Yes, that’s the trigger every time.”\n\nI probably should pay more attention to it. I’m the kind of person who makes spreadsheets for campaigns and client timelines, but apparently not for my own life, which is ridiculous. So… I guess my answer is I’m not sure, but stress seems like it might be part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever followed a very strict diet or restricted your food intake in an attempt to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “very strict,” exactly. I mean, I’ve tried to be more careful before—like deciding I’m going to meal prep, or cut back on certain things, or do one of those plans where you tell yourself you’re being “balanced,” but then suddenly you’re spending half of Sunday chopping vegetables and buying containers you’ll lose the lids to.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess… I don’t know. I’ve attempted to control my weight with food rules at different times, but whether it was very strict? I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clean answer for that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to make up for what you ate during one of these episodes by fasting or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’ve had mornings where I wake up feeling kind of guilty and tell myself, “Okay, just be normal today,” or “don’t overdo it,” but I’m not sure if that counts as fasting or skipping meals.\n\nSometimes breakfast is just coffee because I’m rushing, but that’s also been true since I started working in marketing—half my mornings are me answering emails while standing in the kitchen with one shoe on. So I don’t know if it’s connected to the eating the night before, necessarily.\n\nI guess I can’t say for sure that I deliberately fast to make up for it. I haven’t really paid attention to it in that exact way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I haven’t really noticed myself exercising afterward. I’ll sit there feeling kind of disgusted with myself. But I also don’t exactly announce it.\n\nSometimes I’ll throw packaging away right away or straighten up the kitchen, but honestly I straighten everything up—my desk, my inbox, the pantry shelves if I’m procrastinating—so I don’t know if that “counts” as hiding or if it’s just me being particular. My team jokes that my color-coded project boards could survive a natural disaster, which is… not really relevant, sorry.\n\nSo, I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think anyone is really around to notice most of the time, so I haven’t thought of it as concealing it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable afterward sometimes, like physically and mentally, and then I’ll start doing that spiral where I’m thinking, “Okay, tomorrow I’ll be more structured,” and then I make some overly ambitious plan, like I’m launching a whole product strategy for my own life—which is ridiculous.\n\nBut vomiting or laxatives… I don’t have a clear “yes” there. I’m not sure if you mean ever-ever, or as a pattern after these episodes. It’s not something I’ve noticed myself doing after them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively after one of these nighttime eating episodes to try to burn off the calories?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a deliberate “I have to burn this off” way, at least. I mean, sometimes I’ll tell myself I should be more active, but then that turns into me reorganizing my schedule or looking up workout plans and comparing apps instead of actually doing anything, which is very me.\n\nI used to have phases where I’d get intense about steps or classes, but after these nighttime episodes specifically? I haven’t really noticed myself exercising afterward. Usually it’s late, and I’m tired, and then I’m more likely to just… sit with feeling bad about it than go do a workout.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at those times because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every single time, maybe, but a lot of the worst ones are after a tense night with my husband. Like if we’ve had that kind of cold, clipped conversation where nobody is technically yelling but the whole room feels awful.\n\nAnd then once I start eating, it’s like… the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and at the same time I’m still reaching for more. Which is embarrassing to say out loud.\n\nIt’s strange because at work I make decisions all day—budgets, campaigns, people wanting answers immediately—and I can be very composed. But then at night, after something upsetting, it’s like I can’t get that same part of myself to show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed, especially after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… afterward, definitely. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed in the big official sense, but I feel really low and embarrassed. Kind of disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’ve ruined this whole idea I have of myself as a disciplined person, which is probably dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes. I’ll sit there thinking, “Why didn’t you just go for a run, or do yoga, or literally anything else?” And then I start thinking about all the people at work who see me as organized and put together, and I’m sitting in the kitchen feeling like I’ve completely failed at something very basic.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you've eaten something during one of these episodes and then don't remember eating it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so? Like, I don’t usually find a wrapper and have no memory of eating it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes the whole thing feels kind of blurry afterward, but more in that way where I wish I hadn’t been doing it, not like I blacked out. I’ll remember the general… the standing in the kitchen, opening things, picking at something. Although then I start thinking about how many times I open the pantry for completely unrelated reasons, like looking for the dog’s treats or realizing we’re out of paper towels, and it all gets mixed together in my head.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve had the experience of eating something and truly not remembering it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed right as you're finishing eating during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… especially right as it’s ending, or just after. I get this awful embarrassed feeling, like, “What did I just do?” It’s not even only about the food, it’s like I’ve blown this whole image I have of being disciplined and in control.\n\nAnd then my mind starts doing that annoying comparison thing—like, I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or just gone upstairs and brushed my teeth, you know? I’ll sit there feeling kind of disgusted with myself. Which sounds harsh, but that’s honestly where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during those nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the worst part. Once I start, especially if it’s been one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like the part of me that’s supposed to make decisions just sort of… shuts off.\n\nI’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’m still reaching for more anyway. It’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing, exactly. I do. But in that moment it feels really hard to pull myself out of it.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about how ridiculous it is because at work I can manage ten different deadlines and a whole team, and then at home I can’t seem to stop myself in front of the pantry. So yes, I do feel unable to stop once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like once I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband, the decision-making part of my brain just goes offline.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop, that’s enough,” and then my hand is still going back for more. And it’s not even always that I wanted that specific thing, if that makes sense… it’s more like I’m just in the pantry or kitchen and continuing. Like I’ve lost the thread of what I meant to do.\n\nSorry, I know I’m probably over-explaining. But yes, in those moments I don’t feel in control of what or how much I’m eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the stress or anxiety you feel, especially after arguments with your husband, is what usually triggers these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I’m not totally sure I can say it’s usually that. The arguments definitely leave me feeling wound up, but I haven’t really tracked it in a neat way, you know?\n\nSometimes by nighttime I’m just carrying the whole day around with me—work stuff, little household things, that feeling like I didn’t get a minute to myself. And then if there was tension with my husband, it’s sort of… added on top. But I don’t know if I can honestly say, “Yes, that’s the trigger every time.”\n\nI probably should pay more attention to it. I’m the kind of person who makes spreadsheets for campaigns and client timelines, but apparently not for my own life, which is ridiculous. So… I guess my answer is I’m not sure, but stress seems like it might be part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever followed a very strict diet or restricted your food intake in an attempt to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “very strict,” exactly. I mean, I’ve tried to be more careful before—like deciding I’m going to meal prep, or cut back on certain things, or do one of those plans where you tell yourself you’re being “balanced,” but then suddenly you’re spending half of Sunday chopping vegetables and buying containers you’ll lose the lids to.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess… I don’t know. I’ve attempted to control my weight with food rules at different times, but whether it was very strict? I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clean answer for that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to make up for what you ate during one of these episodes by fasting or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’ve had mornings where I wake up feeling kind of guilty and tell myself, “Okay, just be normal today,” or “don’t overdo it,” but I’m not sure if that counts as fasting or skipping meals.\n\nSometimes breakfast is just coffee because I’m rushing, but that’s also been true since I started working in marketing—half my mornings are me answering emails while standing in the kitchen with one shoe on. So I don’t know if it’s connected to the eating the night before, necessarily.\n\nI guess I can’t say for sure that I deliberately fast to make up for it. I haven’t really paid attention to it in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that in a clean way. I mean, “made myself vomit” sounds very deliberate, like planning it or doing something specific, and I don’t think I can say that’s something I’ve… clearly done.\n\nI’ve definitely had nights where I felt awful afterward—physically uncomfortable, embarrassed, just wanting to undo the whole thing somehow. But vomiting specifically… I don’t really have a solid answer there. Sorry, I know that’s probably frustrating. My brain is going to, like, every bad night I’ve had and trying to categorize it, which is apparently not a skill I have outside of work reports."
